Document 081: 2027年春闘、初動のモメンタムは現時点で良好 ～労働側、経営側とも昨年からのトーンダウンはみられず～

### Page 1

Economic Trends

2026 年10 月 22 日

1/5 
2027 年春闘、初動のモメンタムは現時点で良好 
～労働側、経営側とも昨年からのトーンダウンはみられず～ 
 経済調査部 シニアエグゼクティブエコノミスト  新家 義貴 
（℡：050-5474-7490）

（要旨） 
○ 連合は2027 年春闘基本構想で、 定昇込みの賃上げ要求を 「６％中心、５％以上」 、ベア等の
賃上げ分を「４％中心、３％以上」とする目安を示した。前年よりも要求姿勢をやや強めて
おり、５％台の賃上げの定着に加え、さらなる引き上げを目指す姿勢がうかがえる。 
○ 経団連も高い賃上げの継続に前向きな姿勢を維持している。経労委報告の骨子案では賃上げ
モメンタムの「浸透・定着」が掲げられ、高い賃上げの継続に加え、中小企業への波及を重
視する姿勢が示されている。 
○ 日本経済研究センターのＥＳＰフォーキャスト調査によると、民間エコノミストは 2027 年
春闘の賃上げ率を4.96％と予想（厚生労働省ベース）。前年から小幅に鈍化するものの、高
い伸びが続く見通しであり、４年連続で５％台の賃上げを実現できるかが焦点となる。 
○ 春先にはイラン情勢悪化による賃上げ鈍化が懸念されていたが、現時点で労使双方の姿勢に
トーンダウンはみられない。企業業績の底堅さや構造的な人手不足が高い賃上げを支えてお
り、2027 年春闘の初動のモメンタムは良好と評価できる。

連合は、「６％中心、５％以上」と昨年からトーンをやや強める 
2027 年春闘の集中回答日は2027 年の３月中旬だが、実際の交渉に向けての動きは既に始まりつ
つある。 
本日、連合は「2027 春季生活闘争基本構想」を公表した。春闘に向けて最初に注目される重要
イベントであり、翌年の春闘に向けた労働組合側の基本理念や方針、重点課題を示すものであ
る。この基本構想をみることで、労働側が翌年の春闘にどの程度強気な姿勢で臨もうとしている
かを早い段階で確認することができ、春闘全体のトーンをつかむうえで重要な材料となる。 
今回の基本構想の最大の注目点は、定期昇給相当分を含めて「６％中心、５％以上」 、ベースア
ップ等の賃上げ分を「４％中心、３％以上」とし、より高い賃上げを目指す姿勢を示したことで
ある。「５％以上」という要求水準自体は４年連続（2023 年は「５％程度」）となるが、今回は
「６％中心」との表現が新たに加わった。ベア等の賃上げ分についても、従来の「３％以上」に

### Page 2

2/5 
「４％中心」が加えられている。前年の2026 年春闘では、全体で「５％以上」 、中小で「６％以
上」 、有期・短時間・契約等労働者では「７％」を目安とする、いわゆる「５・６・７」の要求が
示されていた。今回、全体についても６％を明確に意識する形となったことは、実質的には５％
を最低ラインとしつつ、より高い賃上げを促す姿勢を強めたものと受け取れる。単に高い賃上げ
を続けるだけでなく、その水準をさらに引き上げようとする意欲がうかがえる。

こうした姿勢の背景にあるのが、ここ数年の高い賃上げ実績である。2026 年春闘では連合ベー
スの賃上げ率の実績が5.01％となり、３年連続で５％台の高い賃上げが実現した。５％という水
準がかつてほど実現困難な目標ではなくなりつつあるなか、連合としても前年と同じ要求目安を
繰り返すだけでは、さらなる賃上げを促すメッセージとして十分ではないと判断した可能性があ
る。５％台の賃上げを４年目も維持するだけでなく、６％近辺を意識して賃上げ率のさらなる引
き上げを目指す段階に入ったというメッセージと位置付けられる。 
賃上げを取り巻く環境も、こうした強気の姿勢を支えている。構造的な人手不足が続くなか、
企業にとって人材の確保・定着は引き続き重要な経営課題であり、賃金引き上げの必要性は高
い。また、物価上昇率は2026 年度後半にかけて再び高まるとみられ、家計の生活コスト負担は重
い。名目賃金が増加しても、物価上昇に追いつかなければ生活水準の改善にはつながらない。実
質賃金を安定的にプラスにするためにも、労働側が高い賃上げを求める理由は十分にある。 
加えて、賃上げの広がりという点でも課題は残る。2026 年春闘では中小組合の賃上げ率が
4.69％と高い伸びを確保したものの、全体の5.01％には届かなかった。大企業と中小企業の間に
はなお差があり、正規雇用と非正規雇用の賃金格差も残っている。連合にとっては、高い賃上げ
春闘年 連合の要求目安（定昇相当分込み） 連合ベース 厚労省ベース
2020 ４％程度 1.90％ 2.00％
2021 ４％程度 1.78％ 1.86％
2022 ４％程度 2.07％ 2.20％
2023 ５％程度 3.58％ 3.60％
2024 ５％以上 5.10％ 5.33％
2025 ５％以上 5.25％ 5.52％
2026 ５％以上 5.01％ 5.18％
2027 ６％中心、５％以上 — —
(出所）連合「春季生活闘争基本構想」、連合「春季生活闘争 第7回（最終）回答集計結果」、厚生労働省「民間主要企業春季賃上げ要求・妥結状況」
連合の賃上げ要求目安と春闘賃上げ率
実績

### Page 3

3/5 
を実現するだけでなく、それを幅広い企業や労働者に波及させることも引き続き重要な課題であ
る。今回の基本構想でも、賃金実態を把握できないなどの事情がある中小組合については
「20,000 円以上・６％以上」 、有期・短時間・契約等労働者については「７％」を目安とする方針
が示された。こうした点を踏まえれば、今回の基本構想は、高い賃上げの持続と一段の引き上
げ、そして賃上げの裾野の拡大を目指すものと評価できる。 
また、今回の基本構想では、新たに2030 年の賃金水準を2026 年比で15％程度引き上げる中期
目標も設定された。毎年の賃上げ率だけでなく、中期的に目指す賃金水準を明示することで、高
い賃上げを一過性のものに終わらせず、継続させようとする姿勢がうかがえる。 
なお、近年は個別組合の実際の賃上げ要求率がすでに６％近くに達しており、 「６％中心」とい
う今回の表現には、こうした実態を追認する意味合いもある。今回の基本構想からは、連合の強
い賃上げ意欲が確認できる一方、実際の賃上げ率が前年を上回るかどうかはまた別問題である。
今後は、主要産業別労働組合組織や個別組合の要求水準が前年からどの程度引き上げられるか
（られないか）が注目される。

経営側も賃上げの継続に前向き 
経営側も高い賃上げの継続に前向きな姿勢を維持している。経団連が毎年１月に公表する「経
営労働政策特別委員会報告（経労委報告） 」は、春闘に向けた経営側の基本スタンスを示すもので
あり、賃上げ交渉の方向性を占う上で重要な資料となる。2027 年春闘に向けた経労委報告につい
ては、現時点で原案は明らかになっていないものの、９月上旬には骨子案の内容が報道されてい
る。それによると、賃上げモメンタムの「浸透・定着」を掲げ、高い賃上げの継続と中小企業へ
の波及を促す方針とされる。 
経団連は2023 年を賃上げの「起点」 、2024 年を「加速」 、2025 年を「定着」 、2026 年を「さらな
る定着」の年と位置付けてきた。今回の「浸透・定着」という表現からは、高い賃上げを継続す
るとともに、その動きを中小企業などに広げることを重視する姿勢がうかがえる。また、経団連
の筒井会長は９月７日の記者会見で、 「ベースアップ実施の検討を引き続きスタンダードと位置付
けていきたい」と述べ、賃上げのモメンタムを維持する考えを示している。 
数か月前には、イラン情勢の悪化による原材料費や物流費の上昇が企業収益を圧迫し、2027 年
春闘では経営側が賃上げに慎重な姿勢を強めることも懸念されていた。しかし、現時点で経営側
の賃上げ姿勢にトーンダウンはみられない。 
なお、経労委報告では、原案から正式な報告にかけての修正は例年あまり見られないが、骨子
案から原案にかけては内容が多少修正されることもある。今後、10 月末～11 月中頃までに報道さ
れるとみられる経労委報告の原案が明らかになれば、経営側のスタンスをより具体的に判断でき
るだろう。

エコノミスト予想は4.96％、５％を巡る攻防か 
10 月14 日に日本経済研究センターが公表した「ＥＳＰフォーキャスト調査」では、民間エコノ

### Page 4

4/5 
ミスト 34 人を対象に2027 年春闘の賃上げ率について特別調査を実施している。これによると、
予測値の平均は4.96％となり、2026 年実績の5.18％を 0.22％ポイント下回る見通しとなった。
もっとも、前年から小幅に鈍化するとはいえ、ほぼ５％という歴史的に高い水準であり、2027 年
も高い賃上げが続くとの見方が中心である。なお、ここでの賃上げ率は厚生労働省「民間主要企
業春季賃上げ要求・妥結状況」ベースであり、連合の集計とは対象が異なる。 
予測の内訳をみると、定期昇給分が1.69％、ベースアップ分が3.27％となっており、ベアにつ
いても３％を超える伸びが見込まれている。また、予測値の高位８機関平均は5.23％、低位８機
関平均は4.68％となっている。予測には一定の幅があるものの、高い賃上げが続くという点では
概ね認識は共有されている。

2027 年春闘で賃上げ率が前年からやや鈍化すると予想される背景には、2026 年の物価上昇率が
2025 年より低く、物価高に対応するための賃上げ圧力が幾分弱まることがあると考えられる。ま
た、数年にわたる高い賃上げによって人件費負担が累積していることに加え、イラン情勢悪化を
背景とした原材料費や物流費の上昇も、企業の賃上げ余力を抑制する要因となり得る。一方、企
業業績が足元まで好調を維持していることや、構造的な人手不足と採用競争の激化は引き続き賃
上げを促す要因であり、賃上げ率が大きく鈍化する可能性も低いとみられる。 
ちなみに、昨年の同時期のＥＳＰフォーキャスト調査でも、2026 年春闘の賃上げ率は4.81％
（2025 年10 月調査）と、前年実績からの鈍化が予想されていた。しかし、実際には5.18％と予
想を上回り、３年連続で５％台の賃上げが実現した。今回も前年からの小幅な鈍化が見込まれて
いるが、予測平均は昨年の同時期をやや上回っている。イラン情勢の悪化という新たな懸念材料
があるなかでも、エコノミストの間では高い賃上げが続くとの見方が維持されていることが分か

（注）2027年は予測値、2026年までの実績は厚生労働省「民間主要企業春季賃上げ要求・妥結状況」

### Page 5

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
5/5 
る。 
こうしたなか、予測平均の4.96％について、前年からの「鈍化」を強調するか、5％近傍という
「高水準の持続」を重視するかは、評価が分かれるところだろう。いずれにせよ、2027 年春闘で
は、３年連続で実現した５％台の賃上げを４年目も維持できるか、５％を巡る攻防が一つの焦点
となる。今後、経労委報告原案の判明により経営側の方針が明らかになるほか、主要産業別労働
組合組織の要求方針などが明らかになるにつれて、賃上げ率の見通しもより具体化していくこと
になるだろう。 
なお、この調査とは別に、毎年11 月～12 月にかけて、民間シンクタンク・調査機関から翌年の
春闘賃上げ率の予測が出されることが多い （筆者は 11 月に発表予定） 。 予測をレポートとして作成
することもあれば、 経済見通しレポートのなかで言及されることもあり、春闘賃上げ率の見通しに
ついて、この時期に市場の相場観が徐々に形成されていく。

2027 年春闘の初動は良好 
以上を踏まえると、2027 年春闘の「初動のモメンタム」は、現時点では良好と評価できる。イ
ラン情勢が悪化した数か月前には、原材料費や物流費の上昇による企業収益の圧迫を通じて、
2027 年春闘の賃上げ率が大きく鈍化するとの懸念もあった。しかし、ここまでの動きをみる限
り、労働側、経営側のいずれについても、前年と比べて賃上げ姿勢が慎重化している様子はみら
れない。連合が定昇込み、ベアともに前年より強い要求目安を打ち出し、さらに高い賃上げを志
向する一方、経団連も高い賃上げの継続と中小企業への波及に前向きな姿勢を示している。 
その背景には、原油などの代替調達が進み、資源の調達難が生産活動を大きく制約する事態が
回避されたことや、ＡＩ需要の拡大にも支えられ、企業業績が総じて堅調さを維持していること
があるとみられる。また、構造的な人手不足や採用競争の激化によって、企業が人材確保・定着
のために賃金を引き上げる必要性は依然として高い。物価上昇が続くなか、実質賃金の改善を求
める声が強いことも、高い賃上げに向けての動きを後押ししているとみられる。 
もっとも、原材料費や物流費の上昇による企業収益への悪影響が今後さらに広がる可能性はあ
り、先行きを楽観視することはできない。また、現時点で確認できるのは労使双方の基本的な姿
勢であり、具体的な要求や回答の水準はまだ明らかになっていない。今後は、経団連「経労委報
告」の原案や主要産業別労働組合組織の要求方針、早期に賃上げを表明する企業の動向などを通
じて、今回確認された前向きな姿勢が実際の賃上げにつながるかを見極める必要がある。

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
좋은 실적이 상수인 구간 
SK하이닉스의 26년 3분기 매출액은 26년 2분기 대비 31.9% 증가한 104조 
6,150억원이다. 이전 전망 대비 증가한 수준이다. DRAM, NAND 관련 데이터는 
이전 전망 대비 물량은 유사했지만 가격 상승폭은 예상보다 높았다. 26 년 3분기 
영업이익은 26년 2분기 대비 37.6% 증가한 83.3조원이다. 이전 전망을 상회한 
수준이다. 
실적이 끌고, 수급이 밀고 
지금까지는 높은 실적에 대한 불안보다는 먼 미래에 대한 불확실성을 앞서 주가에 
반영시키는 힘이 더 강하게 작용하다가, 주가가 1,190,000원을 찍은 이후 상승세를 
보이고 있다. 실적을 발표한 10월 28일도 10% 넘게 상승했다. 기대 이상의 실적과 
미국 반도체 시장 분위기가 좋았던 영향이다. 앞으로는 악재에 대해서 민감도가 떨
어지는 구간에 진입할 것으로 전망한다. 지나치게 하락한 주가와 밸류에이션이 버팀
목이 될 것이고, Anthropic의 IPO까지 발표되는 Big Tech 기업들의 실적은 시장의 
우려를 충분히 상쇄할 수 있을 것으로 기대한다. 추가로 진행되는 자사주 매수도 불
안한 수급을 해소할 수 있을 전망이다. 
목표주가는 괴리율을 고려해서 하향 조정 
SK하이닉스에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. 26년에도 AI 서버 중심으로 메모리 
시장은 성장할 것으로 기대하고, 실적 대비 주가는 저평가 국면에 있다고 판단하기 
때문이다. 26년 급격히 하락한 주가는 업황, 펀더멘탈을 설명하기에는 과한 움직임이라 
판단한다. 목표주가는 4,000,000원에서 3,200,000원으로 하향한다. 메모리 시장의 
업황에 대한 우려가 아니라 현재 주가와의 괴리율을 고려한 수준이다. 27년 EPS 
538,187원에 PER 6배를 적용하였다.

26년 3분기, 모든 악재를 잠재운 실적 
SK하이닉스의 26년 3분기 매출액은 26년 2분기 대비 31.9% 증가한 104조 
6,150억원이다.  이전 전망 대비 증가한 수준이다. DRAM, NAND 관련 데이터는 이전 
전망 대비 물량은 유사했지만 가격 상승폭은 예상보다 높았다. 
DRAM 매출액은 26년 2분기 대비 35.4% 증가했다. B/G 는 +11.5%, ASP 는 
+28.2%로 추정했다. 3분기 DRAM 가격 상승률은 경쟁사 대비 높은 수준이다. Server 
DRAM 비중이 2분기 대비 높아진 것과 고객 믹스가 개선된 영향으로 추정한다. 
HBM4 비중은 30%를 상회한 것으로 추정한다. HBM이 3분기 가격에 미친 영향은 
제한적인 수준으로 판단한다.  
NAND 매출액은 26년 2분기 대비 18.2% 증가했다. B/G 는 -1%, ASP는 +26%로 
추정한다. 이전 전망치를 상회했다. SSD 비중은 2분기 75%에서 3분기에는 77%로 
상승했다.  
26년 3분기 영업이익은 26년 2분기 대비 37.6% 증가한 83.3조원이다. 이전 전망을 
상회한 수준이다. DRAM 영업이익은 지난 분기 대비 42.3% 증가한 65.1조원으로 
예상한다. 영업이익률은 전 분기 대비 4.0%p 상승한 83.0%로 추정한다. NAND 
영업이익은 전 분기 대비 23.1% 증가한 18.2조원으로 추정한다. 영업이익률은 전 
분기 대비 2.9%p 높아진 72.4%로 추정한다.

4 │ IBKS RESEARCH 
26년 4분기, DRAM 물량 효과 
SK하이닉스의 26년 4분기 매출액은 26년 3분기 대비 29.4% 증가한 135.3조원으로 
예상한다. 가격상승은 지속될 것으로 예상하지만 26년 3분기 대비 상승폭이 낮아질 
전망이다. 물량은 3분기 대비 25% 증가할 것으로 전망한다. 신규 라인 가동 영향이다. 
4분기 영업이익은 26년 3분기 대비 34.1% 증가한 111.7조원으로 예상한다. DRAM 
90.9조원, NAND 20.7조원이다.  
DRAM 매출액은 26년 3분기 대비 36.4% 증가한 107조원으로 예상한다. ASP 는 
+15%, Bit Growth는 +25.0%로 가정하였다. HBM 출하는 지난 분기 대비 증가할 
것으로 예상하고, HBM4 비중은 60%대로 높아질 전망이다. 
26년 4분기 NAND 매출액은 26년 3분기 대비 11.6% 증가할 것으로 예상한다. Bit 
Growth는 +5%로 전망하고, ASP는 +12%로 예상한다. SSD 비중은 소폭 개선될 
전망이다. SSD 내에서 eSSD 비중은 90% 수준이다.

IBKS RESEARCH │5 
공정 전환 속도는 예상보다 빠르게 진행 중: 1cb nm, 321L 
SK하이닉스는 1c nm 비중을 빠르게 확대하고 있다. Wafer 투입이 제한된 상황에서 
공급 확대를 위한 최선의 방법이기 때문이다. HBM 수요는 1b nm를 중심으로 대응할 
예정이다. 이와 관련된 물량도 꾸준히 증가하지만, 출하 Bit 기준으로 1c nm 비중이 
26년 1분기 3% 수준에서 26년 3분기에는 20%, 연말에는 33%까지 높아질 전망이다. 
HBM이 아닌 DRAM 물량이 크게 증가할 전망이다. 이를 통해서 높아진 DRAM 수요 
대응할 것으로 예상한다. 26년 출하 Bit Growth는 28.5%로 상향되었다. 
NAND 생산라인의 공정 전환은 High-End로 꾸준히 변화 중이다. 321L 는 26년 
2분기에는 176L와 Cross over 되었다. 예상보다 1분기 빠른 속도다. 26년 3분기 321L 
비중은 50%이다. 26년말까지 321L를 64%까지 확대할 계획이다.

SK하이닉스 (000660)

6 │ IBKS RESEARCH 
Check Point 
1. DRAM ASP의 차별화 
26년 3분기 DRAM ASP는 메이커별로 차별화가 크다. 26년 2분기 대비 상승폭은 마
이크론이 10% 후반, 삼성전자는 20% 초반으로 추정하고, SK하이닉스는 20% 후반이
다. LTA, HBM 등 다양한 변수가 있지만 SK하이닉스는 고객 비중 변화가 가장 큰 변
수라고 설명한다.  
2. HBM4 물량, 수율 문제 
26년 3분기에 가장 시끄러웠던 변수가 SK하이닉스의 HBM4 관련 품질 문제이다. 26
년 3분기 DRAM 매출과 영업이익으로는 문제가 있었다고 판단하기 어렵다. 26년 3분
기 HBM에서 HBM4 비중은 30%를 상회한 것으로 추정되고 4분기 말에는 비중이 60%
까지 높아질 것으로 전망한다.   
3. HBM 8Hi 확대가 시장 규모 감소는 아니다. 
HBM4는 12Hi가 원래 제품 사양이다. HBM 공급이 부족해지면서 GPU를 좀 더 많이 
팔기 위해서 적용되는 HBM 용량을 줄이는 방향으로 제품 설계가 변경되는 중이다. 
2027년 하반기에는 8Hi 비중이 12Hi 비중을 상회할 것으로 예상한다. 하지만 이로 
인해서 HBM 전체 물량이 감소하는 것은 아닌 것으로 전망하고 있다.  
4. 투자와 생산은 다르다 
SK하이닉스는 용인팹을 공기를 앞당겨서 완공할 계획이다. 이로 인해서 공급량이 예
상 대비 커지는 것에 대한 우려가 있지만 건물 완공과 장비 반입에는 시차가 있고, 수
요를 고려하지 않은 장비 반입은 없을 것으로 밝혔다. 
5. 주주환원. 더 남아 있다 
공시를 통해서 26년 회계연도 기준 약 100조원의 주주환원 계획을 밝혔다. 배당을 30
조원 이상, 자사주 매입/소각을 60조원이상을 추진할 계획이다. 26년 4분기 중에 배
당은 14조원을 집행하고, 자사주는 20조원을 매입할 예정이다. 자사주 20조원 중에서 
10조원은 종업원 상여금 지급용이어서 주주환원에 포함되지 않는다.  
26년 4분기 실적 발표에서 추가적인 배당과 자사주 매입에 대한 계획을 발표할 것으
로 예상한다.

ASP 움직임은 메이커별로

IBKS RESEARCH │7 
펀더멘탈과 센티멘탈의 줄다리기 구간 
SK하이닉스에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. AI 서버 중심으로 메모리 시장은 
성장할 것으로 기대하고, 실적 대비 주가는 저평가 국면에 있다고 판단하기 때문이다. 
26년 급격히 하락한 주가는 업황, 펀더멘탈을 설명하기에는 과한 움직임이라 판단한다.  
목표주가는 4,000,000원에서 3,200,000원으로 하향한다. 메모리 시장의 업황에 대한 
우려가 아니라 현재 주가와의 괴리율을 고려한 수준이다. 2027년 EPS 538,187원에 PER 
6배를 적용하였다.  
아직 제대로 보급되지 않은 Agent AI가 확산되면 컴퓨팅 파워 필요성에 대한 인식도 
크게 바뀔 것으로 전망한다. 메타의 MUSE는 가입자당 8GB의 DRAM을 가정하고 
있다. Open AI, Google, Anthropic 에서도 Agent Platform이 공개된다면 필요한 
DRAM은 폭발적으로 증가할 것으로 전망한다. 
메모리 수요에 대한 규모가 너무 커지는 것이 걱정이지 누가 살 것인지에 대한 것은 
걱정할 문제가 아니라 판단한다. 기존 AI 모델의 진화, 효율적 모델의 등장, Agent AI 
모델의 확대 모두 메모리 수요를 기하급수적으로 확장시키는 변수이다. 
투자 주체들의 자금난이 수요를 저해하는 변수는 아닌 것으로 판단한다. 대체 
투자자들은 지속적으로 증가할 것으로 전망하기 때문이다.  
메모리 업체들의 투자 계획은 수요에 대응하기 위한 투자일 뿐 공급과잉을 만들 
수준은 아닐 것으로 판단한다.

그림 5. SK하이닉스 투자자별 순매수 추이(2010년부터 누계)  그림 6. SK하이닉스 마이크론 밸류에이션 비교

자료: 에프앤가이드 QuantiWise, IBK투자증권  자료: Bloomberg, IBK투자증권

Document 272: AI・半導体で韓国景気は堅調も、「Ｋ字型」の様相が一段と鮮明に ～家計部門の低迷が李政権の重しとなる可能性～

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI・半導体で韓国景気は堅調も、「Ｋ字型」の様相が一段と鮮明に ～家計部門の低迷が李政権の重しとなる可能性～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-27
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 27 日

1/5 
AI・半導体で韓国景気は堅調も、「Ｋ字型」の様相が一段と鮮明に 
～家計部門の低迷が李政権の重しとなる可能性～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ 韓国景気は、世界的なＡＩ・半導体関連需要を追い風に、外需主導で堅調な推移が続いてい
る。 ７-９月の実質ＧＤＰ成長率は前期比＋1.0％ （年率換算＋3.93％） 、 前年同期比＋3.4％
となり、2026 年９月までの累計成長率（＋3.6％）は中銀の８月時点の見通し（＋3.3％）を
上回った。メモリ半導体を中心とする財輸出に加え、半導体製造設備やデータセンター関連
の設備投資が成長を支え、2027 年も外需の堅調さが景気をけん引する展開が見込まれる。 
○ 一方、家計部門は力強さを欠いている。インフレ率は中銀目標の２％を上回って推移し、政
府の現金給付などの消費支援も効果が一巡したことから、個人消費は下振れた。成果給の恩
恵は一部の労働者にとどまり、業種間の賃金格差の拡大も懸念される。ソウルを中心に不動
産価格の上昇が続くなか、 家計債務を巡る金融リスクもくすぶっており、 中銀は10 月会合で
政策金利を据え置きつつも、追加利上げに含みを持たせた。KOSPI はピークから約４割下落
しており、金融市場の調整が家計に与える影響にも注意が必要である。 
○ 為替市場では、株価調整とは対照的に、ウォン相場が底堅く推移している。過去最高を更新
した貿易黒字に伴う実需と、中銀のタカ派姿勢が下支えとなっている。 
○ 政治面では、李在明政権の支持率が過去最低に近い水準にある。企業と家計、資産保有層と
それ以外の層との間で景気回復の恩恵に格差が生じる「Ｋ字型」の様相が強まっており、金
融市場の混乱が家計部門に波及すれば、政権の求心力が一段と低下する可能性がある。

【世界的なAI・半導体需要の旺盛さを追い風に、足元の韓国景気は堅調な推移が続いている】 
韓国では、 世界的なＡＩ （人工知能） ・ 半導体関連需要の旺盛な動きが景気の追い風となる展開が
続いている（注１）。韓国は、DRAM やNAND 型フラッシュメモリ、広帯域メモリ（ＨＢＭ）などメモ
リ半導体の大規模量産で世界トップクラスの競争力を有する。旺盛な半導体需要をけん引役に９月
の輸出額は過去最高額を更新するなど、輸出の勢いは強まっている。韓国はＧＤＰに占める輸出の

注１ 10 月１日付レポート「韓国､AI・半導体需要が景気に追い風も､政治への逆風は止まず」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666170.html）

### Page 2

2/5 
比率が４割を上回るなど、 アジア太平洋
地域のなかでも外需依存度が相対的に
高い。こうしたなか、 ７-９月の実質ＧＤ
Ｐ成長率は前期比＋1.0％、年率換算ベ
ースでは＋3.93％となり、 景気の堅調さ
が確認されている （図１）。 中期的な基調
を示す前年同期比ベースの成長率も＋
3.4％と前期（同＋3.7％）から伸びが鈍
化しているものの、 ３四半期連続で３％
を上回る水準で推移している。 
世界的なＡＩ・半導体関連需要の拡大
を反映して財輸出が押し上げられるとともに（図２）、中国人観光客を中心とする来訪者数の増加
はサービス輸出を押し上げるなど、堅調な外需が景気をけん引する動きがみられる。さらに、 ＡＩ ・
半導体関連需要の旺盛さを追い風に、 半導体製造設備やデータセンター関連の投資が拡大するなど、
設備投資も景気を下支えしている。ま
た、政府消費の増加も成長に寄与した。
一方で、政府はイラン情勢の悪化を受け
た原油高の影響軽減を目的に、低所得者
層などを対象に１人当たり 10 万～60 万
ウォンの現金給付を実施したほか、 省エ
ネ家電の購入促進策 （キャッシュバック
の実施）などの消費支援に動いた。しか
し、足元ではその効果が一巡しており、
個人消費は下振れしている。さらに、前
期は在庫調整が進む動きが確認された
ものの、 当期は前期比年率ベースで＋0.32pt と試算されるなど、在庫の積み上がりを示唆する動き
もうかがえる。 
とはいえ、2026 年の９月までの累計ベースの経済成長率は＋3.6％となり、中銀が８月時点で示
した見通し （＋3.3％）を上回っている。 中銀は10 月会合において、2026 年の経済成長率が８月時
点の見通しを大きく上回る可能性を示唆したが、 足元の状況はそうした見方に沿った動きと捉えら
れる。 さらに、2027 年についても、 世界的なＡＩ・半導体関連需要の動向に留意する必要があるも
のの、外需の堅調さが景気をけん引する展開が続く可能性が見込まれる。

### Page 3

3/5 
【家計部門はインフレに直面、賃金上昇の恩恵は一部にとどまり、不動産高騰や株価調整も重し】 
外需は堅調な動きをみせる一方、対照的に個人消費は力強さを欠いている。背景には、2025 年後
半以降のインフレ率が中銀目標（２％）を上回る伸びで推移するなど、実質購買力に下押し圧力が
かかっていることがある。さらに、イラ
ン情勢の悪化を受けた 原油高を背景に
物価上昇圧力が残り、インフレ率は中銀
目標を上回る水準で推移している （図
３） 。これを受けて、政府は燃料価格の上
限規制 を約６ヵ月維持するなど物価対
策に動いている。しかし、足元では中東
情勢 の不透明 感を理由に 原油価格は高
止まりするなど、見通しが立たない状況
が続いている。一方で、半導体産業など
一部企業では、営業利益などの一定割合
（Ｎ％）を従業員の成果報酬の原資とする「Ｎ％成果給」が導入され、他産業にも導入を求める動
きが広がっている。 とはいえ、実際に高額な成果報酬の恩恵を受ける労働者は一部にとどまるなど、
業種間の賃金格差の拡大に繋がる懸念も高まっている。 
韓国ではここ数年、首都ソウルを中心とする不動産価格の高騰が社会問題化してきた。 政府は
2025 年以降に 様々な不動産高騰対策を
打ち出したものの、一連の対策では実需
層の資金調達が困難になる一方、規制対
象外の地域で不動産価格が上昇するな
ど「副作用」も顕在化した。また、政府
は超高額住宅や所有者が居住していな
い住宅に対する課税強化を目指したも
のの、中高所得者層などからの反発を受
けて一部見直しを余儀なくされた。さら
に、一連の政府主導による不動産対策に
対する不満は、６月に実施されたソウル
市長選で与党候補が敗北した一因との見方もある。とはいえ、 足元においてもソウルを中心とする
不動産価格は上昇が続いており （図４） 、住宅関連融資の増加が続くなど、 家計債務を巡る金融リス
クもくすぶっている。中銀は、10 月の定例会合で３会合ぶりに政策金利を据え置いたものの、 追加
利上げに含みを持たせる考えを示した（注２）。背景のひとつには、 不動産市場を巡るリスクがある

注２ 10 月22 日付レポート「韓国中銀、株価急落などで利上げ休止も、金融引き締めの長期化を示唆」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666810.html）

4/5 
と考えられる。 
2025 年以降の韓国金融市場では、世界的なＡＩ・半導体関連需要の旺盛な動きを追い風に、主要
株価指数（KOSPI）は上昇基調を強めた。
さらに、 政府は2025 年末、 個人投資家が
保有する海外株式を売却して国内株式
に１年間投資した場合、一定の条件の下
で譲渡所得税を最大 100％控除する時限
措置を発表し、2026 年３月に法制化し
た。そのうえ、株価上昇を受けて個人投
資家の間でレバレッジ投資が活発化し
たことも、株価上昇の動きを後押しする
一助となった。しかし、構成銘柄全体の
時価総額に占める割合が５割前後を占
めるサムスン電子とＳＫハイニックスの２社の株価動向にKOSPI が左右されやすい構造にある。 こ
うしたなか、足元ではピークから約４割下落するなど、市場は大幅な調整に直面している（図５）
（注３）。こうした金融市場の不安定な動きも、家計部門に悪影響を与えている可能性がある。

【株価の大幅調整が続いているものの、貿易黒字と中銀のタカ派姿勢がウォン相場を下支え】 
金融市場においては、前述の通り半導体関連株を中心とする株価調整の動きに直面しているもの
の、 通貨ウォン相場は対照的に底堅い動きをみせている （図６） 。 背景には、世界的なＡＩ・半導体
関連需要の旺盛さが輸出を押し上げる
なか、足元の貿易黒字は過去最高を更新
するなど、ウォンに対する実需が生まれ
やすい環境にあることが挙げられる。ま
た、中銀が断続的な利上げを実施すると
ともに、先行きも追加利上げに含みを持
たせるなど「タカ派」姿勢を強めている
ことがある。さらに、 中銀の申総裁は10
月の定例会合後に行った記者会見にお
いて、「足元のウォンの対ドル相場は歴
史的に見れば低水準」 、 「実質実効ベース
でもウォンは依然割安」、「ウォン高は経済的不平等を是正する効果がある」と述べるなど、ウォン
高の経済的な利点を指摘したことも、ウォン相場を後押ししている可能性がある。

注３ 10 月15 日付レポート「韓国株が急落、AI・半導体好況の陰で市場の調整が深まる」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666600.html）

### Page 5

経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。
5/5
【金融市場の混乱や家計部門を取り巻く環境が李在明政権の行方を左右する可能性に要注意】
こうしたなか、李在明（イ・ジェミョン）政権の支持率は低水準で推移しており、足元では過去
最低に近い水準で推移している。 このところの支持率低下を巡っては、 若年層などで支持が弱まる
動きがみられるほか、 不動産政策や物価高への不満も影響していると考えられる。 こうした動きは、
政権が支持層をつなぎとめることの難しさを示唆している。足元の景気はいわゆる「Ｋ字型」の様
相を強めるとともに、 企業と家計、 資産保有層とそれ以外の層との間で景気回復の恩恵に格差が生
じており、その格差が一段と拡大する兆しもみられる。このため、先行きは実体経済の堅調さにも

かかわらず、李政権を取り巻く環境が厳しさを増す可能性も考えられる。与党・共に民主党を巡っ
ても、党内では政策運営などを巡る意見対立がみられるなど、火種を抱えている。金融市場の混乱
が家計部門に波及することで、 李政権の求心力をさらに弱める可能性がある点には注意が必要であ
る。
以 上

### Page 4

Inflation in Australia remains well above target and a number of upside risks have crystallised
since the August Statement. In particular, materially higher global energy prices as a result of
the Middle East conflict have added to inflationary pressures. Government bond yields in
advanced economies have risen to their highest levels in many years, reflecting a combination
of higher term premia, higher neutral rates and expectations of higher policy rates for a time in

response to global inflationary pressures. Domestically, housing market conditions have eased
and the unemployment rate has risen further than anticipated. However, a range of data,
including persistently strong inflation and growth in a broad range of costs, suggest that
domestic capacity pressures have been a little greater than previously assessed.
The outlook for inflation has been revised materially higher and reflects a higher assumed path
for energy prices amid the ongoing Middle East conflict and greater domestic capacity

pressures. Headline and underlying inflation are expected to remain above 3 per cent until
end-2027, some time later than forecast in August, and reach the midpoint of the 2–3 per cent
range in mid-2028. The risks to the inflation outlook are judged to remain skewed to the
upside reflecting risks posed by the Middle East conflict. Tighter monetary policy alongside a
weaker outlook for the housing market is expected to keep GDP growth below trend. The

unemployment rate is projected to reach a bit over 5 per cent at the end of 2028.
The Monetary Policy Board decided it was appropriate to increase the cash rate target by
25 basis points to 4.85 per cent. Inflation remains elevated and recent data indicate that it is
broadly based. The Board noted the increasing risk that higher inflation expectations will
become embedded and judged that a further tightening in financial conditions is warranted
to support the return of inflation to target.

Globally, higher energy prices and resilient demand growth have
added to inflationary pressures, and most advanced-economy
central banks have either raised policy rates or are expected to.
The ongoing Middle East conflict has pushed up energy prices. As a result, headline
inflation has increased across most economies. Prices for a range of base metals and
agricultural commodities have also risen sharply in recent months, driven by the conflicts in

the Middle East and Ukraine and adverse weather.
Global economic demand has also been more resilient than expected, supported by the
AI boom overseas. GDP growth in Australia’s major trading partners has trended higher over
recent years. Recently, the boost to trading partner growth from AI-related demand and
supportive fiscal measures in many economies has offset the adverse effects of the Middle East
conflict. Market pricing implies that most advanced economy central banks are expected to

increase policy rates at least twice by the end of 2027, in line with higher near-term
inflationary pressures.
Overview
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 1

### Page 5

This in part explains why government bond yields have risen to
multi-year highs.
Against the backdrop of stronger global inflationary pressures, government bond
yields have risen since the August Statement, to their highest level in many years,
including in Australia. This reflects a range of factors including expectations of tighter
monetary policy in the near term given global capacity and inflationary pressures, as well as
some combination of higher neutral interest rates and higher term premia. Long-term inflation

expectations in financial markets remain consistent with central bank targets.
Growth in Australia’s major trading partners is still expected to moderate over the next
couple of years as the boost from AI-related activity slows and growth in China eases.
Headline inflation is expected to remain above central bank targets into 2027 in many
advanced economies, reflecting higher energy and other commodity prices, resilient demand
and rapid growth in AI-related prices. Risks to global inflation are judged to remain skewed to

the upside.
Domestically, growth in activity has slowed, housing market
conditions have eased further, and the unemployment rate has
risen by more than expected.
In Australia, GDP growth has slowed to a little below its estimated potential growth
rate, largely reflecting softer growth in household consumption – consistent with the
earlier easing in growth in household income and wealth – exports and public demand.
Notwithstanding this slowing in growth, recent data suggest that activity growth this year has

been a little stronger than anticipated in the August Statement, supported by rapid growth in
data centre investment.
By contrast, conditions in the housing market have eased further. Housing prices in September
were almost 5 per cent below their March peak, reflecting higher interest rates and tax
changes announced in the federal budget. The unemployment rate rose by more than
expected to average 4.6 per cent in the September quarter. That appears to largely reflect a

stronger-than-expected pick-up in labour force participation amid cost-of-living pressures,
while employment growth has held up.
But inflation has been higher than expected, cost pressures
remain elevated, and capacity constraints are now assessed to
have been a little tighter than previously thought.
Headline inflation was 3.9 per cent over the year to the September quarter and
trimmed mean inflation was 3.7 per cent. While the price of fuel has increased significantly,

inflation has been broad-based, with sizeable price increases for a range of market services.
Liaison contacts across most industries continue to report sustained cost pressures.

Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 2

### Page 6

The persistence of high inflation and cost pressures, despite the easing in GDP growth and the
rise in unemployment, suggests that domestic capacity constraints have been tighter than
previously assessed. Reflecting this, the staff’s assessment of the unemployment rate
consistent with low and stable inflation has increased a little, though this assessment remains
highly uncertain.
Financial conditions appear to be restrictive, which is expected to

weigh on domestic capacity pressures.
Financial conditions are judged to be restrictive. There is, however, significant uncertainty
around the extent of this restrictiveness given conflicting evidence. Supporting this
assessment, the current level of the cash rate is slightly above the top of the range of central
estimates of the neutral rate, and the expected cash rate path is higher still. Housing prices
have declined and new housing lending has fallen sharply. However, business credit growth

remains strong and corporate risk premia remain low.
The Australian dollar has appreciated since the start of the year and is judged to be around
estimates of its long-run equilibrium.
Ahead of the November meeting, market pricing was implying expectations of around one
and a half further cash rate increases by mid-next year.
Inflation is expected to remain above 3 per cent until late 2027,
reflecting recent increases in fuel and other conflict-related costs

and greater domestic capacity pressures.
The outlook for inflation has been revised materially higher relative to the August
Statement, particularly through to late 2027. Fuel and other conflict-related costs have
increased by more than expected since August, owing to the ongoing conflict in the Middle
East. Some of these additional costs are expected to be passed on to consumer prices, while
elevated prices of petrol and diesel will also contribute directly to higher headline inflation in

the near term. The stronger outlook for underlying inflation also reflects the staff’s judgement
that capacity pressures have been a little greater this year than thought in August. Headline
and trimmed mean inflation are now expected to remain above 3 per cent until end-2027,
some time later than forecast in August, and to return to 2.5 per cent in mid-2028.
The risks to the inflation outlook remain skewed to the upside.
Some of the upside risks identified in August have materialised and are now

incorporated in the central forecast, including greater domestic capacity pressures and
higher oil prices. But upside risks to inflation from the Middle East conflict and AI-related
investment are judged to remain. Energy prices could stay at current elevated levels or rise
further, rather than decline as assumed, if disruptions to shipping and refining persist.
Combined with ongoing capacity pressures in the domestic economy and elevated inflation

expectations, this would increase the risk of stronger pass-through to consumer prices. Strong
AI-related investment activity could also push up input costs by more than expected, both
directly and by adding to capacity pressures.
OverviewOverviewOverviewOverviewOverviewOverviewOverview
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 3

### Page 7

However, inflation could fall faster than forecast. For example, capacity pressures could ease
sooner if higher interest rates, conflict-related cost pressures and uncertainty, and falling
housing prices combine to weigh more heavily than expected on spending and investment.
In Australia, tighter monetary policy is expected to keep GDP
growth below potential, with spare capacity emerging in the
labour market in 2027.
Higher-than-expected global energy prices, a softer housing market and a higher

assumed path for the cash rate than in the August forecasts are expected to constrain
domestic GDP growth. The very strong expansion in data centre investment is expected to
only partly offset these headwinds to growth.
Labour market conditions are expected to ease. The unemployment rate is forecast to rise
to a bit over 5 per cent by end-2028. That is consistent with below-potential GDP growth but is
around 1⁄4 percentage point higher than previously expected, largely reflecting the judgement

that more people than previously forecast will remain in or join the labour force. The economy
is currently operating with excess demand and is judged to reach balance next year, slightly
later than previously thought, with spare capacity emerging in 2027.
The Monetary Policy Board decided to increase the cash rate
target.
Inflation remains elevated and data since the previous meeting have confirmed that
underlying inflation remains broadly based. Capacity pressures appear a little tighter than

previously estimated and there are indications that higher fuel prices are being passed
through to prices of other goods and services. Inflation is now expected to remain above
3 per cent for the coming year and there is an increasing risk that higher inflation expectations
will become embedded. Given the revised inflation outlook and the continuing upside risks,
the Board judged that a further tightening in financial conditions is warranted to support the

return of inflation to target. The Board stated that it will continue to do what it considers
necessary to bring inflation sustainably back to target.

### Page 9

Summary
Chapter 1
Financial Conditions
• Financial conditions are judged to be restrictive, but with various cross-currents
making it difficult to determine the extent of this. Over the course of this year, the rise
in the cash rate target and the expected path of rates, along with higher term premia, have
contributed to tighter financial conditions. And while some estimates of the neutral rate
have also risen, the cash rate and market-implied path are still a little above the top of the

range of central estimates of neutral. Also, housing prices have declined by more than
expected and new housing lending has declined sharply in response to tighter monetary
policy and recent tax changes. Meanwhile, the nominal exchange rate has appreciated
since the start of the year but remains broadly in line with estimates of its long-run
equilibrium relationship. By contrast, business credit growth remains very strong and
corporate risk premia are very low. Moreover, funding remains readily available for banks

and most household and business borrowers.
• Overnight indexed swap (OIS) pricing implies market participants assign an 85 per
cent probability to a cash rate increase in November, with around one and a half
cash rate increases priced in by mid-2027. Expectations for the cash rate have increased
since the August Statement. This reflects stronger-than-expected domestic inflation data,
higher oil prices, the September cash rate increase, and RBA communications that on

balance were interpreted as hawkish. Almost all market economists tracked by the staff
expect a cash rate increase in November.
• Most advanced economy central banks are expected to raise policy rates further
over the next year. Since the August Statement, the US Federal Reserve (Fed), European
Central Bank (ECB), Bank of Japan (BoJ), Bank of Canada (BoC), Reserve Bank of New
Zealand (RBNZ), Norges Bank and Bank of Korea (BoK) increased their policy rates, while the

Bank of England (BoE) left its policy rate unchanged but retained a tightening bias. These
actions, and the increase in market-implied future policy paths since August, reflect the
re-escalation of the Middle East conflict and, in some economies, persistent inflationary
pressures stemming from capacity constraints. Market pricing implies that most advanced
economy central banks are expected to increase policy rates at least twice by the end
of 2027.

Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 6

### Page 10

• Government bond yields have increased notably across advanced economies in
recent months, in many cases reaching their highest levels in many years, including
in Australia. Higher yields reflect expectations of tighter monetary policy in the near term,
higher neutral interest rates and higher term premia. The increase in longer-term yields has
occurred at a time of strong public and private sector debt issuance, and models suggest
that roughly half of the rise in yields is due to term premia and half to perceptions of

neutral (although there is considerable uncertainty around these estimates). Long-term
inflation expectations remain consistent with central bank targets even though
shorter-term expectations are elevated. While sovereign debt markets have continued to
function well across major advanced economies, bond market volatility has increased in
recent weeks and the rise in bond yields in some European countries has been large
(particularly France, amid fiscal concerns).

• In corporate funding markets, equity and credit risk premia remain low for
higher-quality firms but have risen for lower-rated and more interest-rate-sensitive
firms. Spreads on investment-grade corporate bonds, including in Australia, remain
around historical lows despite strong issuance, while sub-investment grade spreads have
widened but remain very low in historical terms. In major equity markets, strong gains in
energy stocks and some IT stocks since the August Statement have offset declines in most

other sectors, though investors are differentiating more between AI-related firms.
Australian equity prices have declined in response to the rise in interest rates and as the
downturn in the housing market has lowered earnings expectations in some key sectors.

Financial conditions in Australia are
assessed to be restrictive, but with
various cross-currents making it
difficult to determine the extent of
this.
The cash rate is slightly above the range of
model-based and market economist estimates of
neutral, and the expected cash rate path based on
market pricing is higher still, suggesting that policy
is restrictive. Other indicators paint a mixed picture. For
example, housing market conditions have weakened by

more than the tightening in monetary policy would
suggest and funding conditions for property developers
have tightened, suggesting that financial conditions are
clearly restrictive. At the same time, however, funding
conditions for most other businesses remain favourable
and do not suggest that conditions are particularly
restrictive. Table 1.1 summarises evidence contributing
to this assessment.

1.1 Assessment of financial conditions

### Page 12

There are some tentative signs that neutral rates
may be rising, globally and in Australia. Since the
pandemic, estimates of neutral rates in advanced
economies have increased by 1–3 percentage points,
and the recent sharp increase in long-term global bond
yields is consistent with market participants revising
their estimates of the global neutral rate higher still (see
Box A: How Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields
Affect Financial Conditions?). As a small open economy,

Australia’s neutral rate is likely to be significantly
influenced by neutral rates globally (see Box B:
Developments in the Neutral Rate of Interest). Consistent
with that, some of our estimates of neutral and those of
market economists have increased since the August
Statement, though the average of all measures is not
much changed (Graph 1.1).

Suite of models
Nominal Neutral Rate
By itself, a rising neutral rate would imply that any
given path for policy rates is somewhat less
restrictive than previously assessed. However, the
cash rate and market path have also increased since the

August Statement, and term premia estimates are higher.
Long-term inflation expectations are little changed and
consistent with the inflation target, suggesting that
market participants expect policy will be set at a level
that is sufficiently restrictive to contain
inflationary pressures.
Other indicators of financial conditions provide a
mixed picture. Bank lending rates and short-term bond
yields have evolved broadly in line with increases in the

cash rate and its expected path. Housing prices and new
lending have declined by more than expected,
indicating tight financial conditions for the housing
sector in response to monetary policy tightening and tax
changes. By contrast, strong business credit growth and
generally very low corporate borrowing spreads and
equity risk premia suggest that financial conditions are
not overly restraining aggregate business funding
(though conditions have tightened considerably in

some sectors, most notably housing construction).
This assessment of financial conditions is subject to
uncertainty. Elevated global borrowing by the public
and private sector, including to fund AI investments –
and the possibility of stronger AI-led productivity growth
in future – could materially raise global and domestic
neutral rates over time. Conversely, the strong response
of the Australian housing market could indicate that the
tax changes are amplifying the effects of tighter

monetary policy by more than expected, while
weaker-than-expected labour market outcomes suggest
that policy is working to restrain economic activity. Term
premia also appear to have risen, which would tighten
financial conditions at the margin. However, there is
considerable uncertainty around estimates of term
premia, neutral rates and the lags with which changes in
the cash rate affect financial conditions.

Most advanced economy central
banks are expected to tighten policy

further, reflecting inflationary
pressures, including from the effects
of the Middle East conflict.
Since the August Statement, the Fed, ECB, BoJ, BoC,
RBNZ, Norges Bank and BoK all increased their
policy rates. Many of these central banks highlighted
the strength or resilience of economic growth and
inflationary pressures from the re-escalation in the
Middle East conflict, with inflation already persistently
above target in some economies. By contrast, the BoE

left its policy rates unchanged, citing a balance between
downside risks to activity and upside risks to inflation.
The BoE indicated that it would tighten policy further if
upside inflation risks were to materialise.
Market-implied policy paths have shifted up in
advanced economies since the August Statement
(Graph 1.3). Market pricing implies policy rates are
expected to increase by around 75 basis points and be
at or above the range of estimates of neutral in many

advanced economies by the end of next year (although
central banks have expressed uncertainty about the level
of neutral) (Graph 1.4). For most economies, much of the
increase in policy rate paths occurred following the
re-escalation in the Middle East conflict.
Stronger-than-expected economic activity and central
bank communication emphasising upside risks to
inflation also contributed to the upward shift in
some economies.

The People’s Bank of China (PBC) has retained a
slight easing bias amid low inflation, and
incrementally eased policy in late September
through structural policy tools. The tools direct
lending to priority sectors, including infrastructure and
manufacturing, and accompanied increased bond
issuance quotas for local governments (see Chapter 2:

Economic Conditions). Authorities have said that they
are concerned that broader monetary easing through a
lower policy rate would pressure bank profitability.
Advanced economy government
bond yields have risen further to be
around their highest level in many
years.
Government bond yields have increased materially
across advanced economies since the August
Statement, notwithstanding some retracement over
recent weeks alongside declines in equity prices.

Shorter-term yields are around 40 basis points higher,
reflecting expectations of monetary policy tightening in
response to near-term inflationary pressure (Graph 1.5).
Longer-term yields have increased by around 30 basis
points for many economies, driven by an increase in real
yields. Most recently, growing concerns about the
sustainability and profitability of capital expenditure
plans at some AI-related firms (see below) has seen
yields decline somewhat as investors have switched into

safer government bonds. Model-based decompositions
attribute roughly half of the increase in the US 10-year
nominal Treasury yield since the August Statement to
higher expected future real policy rates, and the rest to
higher term premia, but there is considerable
uncertainty around these estimates. Box A: How Does an
Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial
Conditions? discusses the recent increase in yields
globally and the implications for financial conditions in

Australia, while Box B: Developments in the Neutral Rate
of Interest discusses the neutral rate and its
longer-term drivers.

Measures of Inflation Compensation
Volatility has increased in many sovereign bond
markets. Concerns about fiscal sustainability in France
saw the spread between 10-year French and German
government bonds rise to around the highest level since
the eurozone crisis (Graph 1.7). However, outside Europe,
market pricing provides little evidence of material
concerns about sovereign credit risk: government bond

auctions have generally remained well supported,
long-term inflation compensation remains anchored
and sovereign credit default swap premia remain low.

10-year Government Bond Yields
In emerging markets, local-currency sovereign bond

yields have risen since the August Statement, though in
many cases by a little less than in advanced markets.
Chinese bond yields remain little changed, underpinned
by low inflation and low policy rates, and with capital
controls reducing the sensitivity of Chinese yields to
movements in global rates. In the rest of emerging Asia,
lower debt-to-GDP ratios in many countries, compared
with advanced economies, may be contributing to
generally more modest yield increases.

Australian government bond yields have also
increased since the August Statement to be around
their highest levels in many years (Graph 1.8). As in
other advanced economies, much of the increase in
longer-term yields has been driven by a rise in real yields,
while measures of longer-term inflation compensation
have been little changed. Market contacts have
attributed the rise in longer-term real yields to a
combination of higher expected policy rates (including

because of a potential rise in the neutral rate) and higher
term premia, although views differ on the relative
importance (the RBA’s model estimates suggest a
roughly even split between the two). In recent weeks,
yields have declined a little alongside similar moves
offshore. The increase in volatility in AGS has been
smaller than in peer economies, and AGS market
functioning remains good.

Risk premia for equities and
investment-grade corporate bonds
remain very low but have increased
for lower-rated bonds.
Equity prices in advanced overseas economies have
been overall little changed since the August
Statement, masking significant divergences across
sectors and economies (Graph 1.9). Higher interest
rates and ongoing tensions in the Middle East have
weighed on some sectors, with consumer discretionary
and industrial stocks – which are more sensitive to

higher yields and energy prices – declining the most
(Graph 1.10). By contrast, energy stocks and some IT
stocks have continued to rise, although investors are
differentiating between technology firms more (see
below). In China, equity prices have declined sharply,
particularly for AI stocks affected by declining data
centre investment sentiment. Meanwhile, earnings
growth in the United States has remained broadly based,
reflecting continued resilience in economic activity, with

growth strongest in AI-related firms. Measures of equity
risk premia remain around long-term lows.

Australian equity prices have declined since the
August Statement, owing to the effect of higher
interest rates and weakness in sectors exposed to the

downturn in the domestic housing market. Share prices
for banks, consumer discretionary and real estate firms
have declined alongside weaker earnings expectations
and a higher expected path for the cash rate, and
suggest that investors expect consumer spending to
remain subdued. Consumer-facing firms continue to
report that households remain value conscious and are
trading down to lower-cost alternatives, weighing on
profitability in discretionary sectors. Profits at consumer

staples firms have been more resilient, while equity
prices for Australian-listed energy companies have
increased alongside higher oil prices. Overall, analysts’
earnings expectations for Australian firms have declined
recently, having trailed other advanced economies for
some years including due to lower prices for key
commodities and subdued economic growth.

Investment grade corporate bond spreads have
increased slightly in advanced economies but
remain around historically low levels (Graph 1.11).
The rise in spreads for sub-investment grade firms has
been more noticeable, in part because their
creditworthiness is more sensitive to higher interest
rates, although spreads remain low. The slight increase in
Australian bond spreads has tightened funding
conditions in Australia at the margin, though funding is

readily available to investment grade firms. Borrowers
reliant on private credit have seen a more significant
tightening of funding conditions, discussed below.

Investors are differentiating between technology
firms more. Large ‘AI hyperscalers’ reported very strong
revenue growth in the latest quarter, but their share
prices and bond spreads have become more sensitive to
substantial upward revisions to planned capital
expenditure and to concerns about whether this
investment will earn adequate returns (Graph 1.12).
Capital expenditure by these firms is now expected to

be around US$800 billion this year and to approach
US$1.4 trillion by the end of 2027 – around 5 per cent of
US GDP and around twice what was expected in late
2025 – with a growing share financed through bond
issuance. Korean equity prices have declined, driven by
falls in the share prices of memory chip producers amid
concerns about the scale and sustainability of the AI
data centre build-out; these concerns have also affected
Chinese AI stocks (Graph 1.13). By contrast, Taiwanese

equities have outperformed those elsewhere in Asia,
partly reflecting stronger earnings expectations for chip
manufacturer TSMC.

Similarly, some Australian data centre firms have
faced increased investor scrutiny. Most notably, AI

data centre developer Firmus withdrew its planned
initial public offering (IPO) in October following weak
investor demand at the proposed valuation, and some
other data centre operators have experienced equity
price falls and a widening in credit spreads;
commentators suggest that the IPO withdrawal may
have been one factor contributing to weaker global
sentiment towards AI-related firms. By contrast, a range
of other Australian data centre operators have seen little

change in their cost of capital.

### Page 18

Low corporate credit and equity risk premia suggest
that investors continue to expect the economic
impact of the Middle East conflict to be limited and
AI investment to ultimately be profitable, among
other things. However, a material reassessment of the
economic viability of AI-related investment, the effects of
the Middle East conflict, US policy uncertainty following
the midterm elections or concerns about fiscal
sustainability globally could lead to a broader weakening

in risk sentiment. This could spill over to Australian
markets via higher risk premia and reduced access to
funding.1

### Page 19

The Australian dollar is little changed
since the August Statement and
remains around 5 per cent higher
than the start of the year.
The Australian dollar TWI is little changed since the
August Statement (Graph 1.14). Australian government
bond yields have risen since August, but major trading
partner yields have risen by a similar, and in some cases
greater, degree. This modest narrowing in yield
differentials has been offset by support from the weaker

euro following concerns regarding fiscal sustainability in
France. Since the start of the year, the Australian dollar
has appreciated by around 5 per cent in TWI terms and
remains broadly consistent with estimates of its long-run
equilibrium level, although there is a degree of
uncertainty around this assessment.

Banks’ funding costs and lending
rates have increased in line with the
cash rate since the beginning of the
year.
Banks’ funding costs rose by around 80 basis points
between January and September, and will rise
further as September’s increase in the cash rate
continues to be passed through (Graph 1.15). Banks
have increased average deposit rates broadly in line with
increases in the cash rate this year. This contrasts with
previous easing and tightening phases, when average

deposit rates have tended to change by less than the
cash rate because some deposits, such as transaction
accounts, do not reprice fully when the cash rate
changes. The stronger-than-usual pass-through this year
is consistent with some banks highlighting strong
competition for deposits over recent quarters. Wholesale
funding conditions remain favourable, with spreads
between bank bond yields and swap rates remaining
low. Bank bond issuance has been a little above average

in 2026.

reflecting a long-run decline in consumer credit relative

Credit Growth by Sector*
New housing loan commitments
have declined sharply, particularly
for investors, and housing credit

growth has eased.
New housing loan commitments have declined
further since the August Statement and are now
27 per cent below their peak in November 2025. The
decline in overall housing loan commitments to date is
larger than most tightening phases since 1990, though
not as large as at the same point in the 2022 tightening
phase (Graph 1.20).4 While the increase in interest rates
over 2026 has weighed on demand from all categories
of borrowers, the decline in new lending has been most

pronounced for investors and has been concentrated in
loans to purchase established dwellings. This is
consistent with the recent changes to negative gearing
and capital gains tax decreasing the after-tax returns on
investments in established housing.

New Housing Commitments
over Tightening Phases*
Housing credit growth is now 6.3 per cent in
six-month-ended annualised terms, lower than at
the time of the August Statement but still around its
post-GFC average. The recent decline has reflected a
fall in investor credit growth; owner-occupier credit
growth has remained little changed in six-month-ended

terms and has softened only slightly in monthly terms, in
line with the more modest decline in owner-occupier
commitments. Housing loan commitments and credit
growth tend to lag housing price growth by up to three
months, largely reflecting lags between the purchase
and settlement of property. As a result, housing credit
growth is expected to decline further over coming
months as recent declines in housing prices and
turnover continue to flow through to credit (see

Chapter 2: Economic Conditions).

### Page 23

Non-bank housing credit is a small share of total
housing credit but has been growing quickly
(Graph 1.21). Growth in non-bank housing credit has
accelerated considerably over the past 18 months,
mainly reflecting new lending rather than refinancing of
bank loans. In part, this growth is likely to reflect less
stringent debt servicing tests at some non-banks
compared with banks (which are required to assess
serviceability at an interest rate that is three percentage

points above the prevailing mortgage rate, reflecting the
Australian Prudential Regulation Authority’s (APRA)
serviceability buffer). Non-bank credit growth has also
been supported by generally favourable conditions in
securitisation markets – a key funding source for these
lenders – allowing non-banks to compete more readily
with banks for customers. Issuance of residential
mortgage-backed securities has been very strong, with
cumulative issuance in the year-to-date (relative to GDP)

exceeding the range seen in recent years (Graph 1.22).
While spreads on residential mortgage-backed securities
have increased recently they remained very narrow in
historical terms.

Growth in business debt remains

As in some overseas jurisdictions, debt financing for
Australian data centre operators has been an

important contributor to strong business debt
growth. In 2026 to date, operators have raised at least
$30 billion in the bond and syndicated loans markets
(domestically and offshore), including syndicates
involving Australian banks. This is equivalent to around
one-fifth of the increase in the stock of business debt so
far this year (Graph 1.24). However, operators may also
use other sources of debt funding such as bilateral bank
loans and private credit. This suggests that the total

volume of borrowing by operators is likely to be higher
than the $30 billion quoted above (though it is unclear
how much higher). So far, the decline in global and
domestic investor sentiment towards some riskier
AI-related firms (discussed above) is having only a
limited effect on broader cost and availability of funding
for most Australian data centre operators. Even so, this
remains a risk if sentiment were to deteriorate further.

Funding conditions for private credit lenders have
tightened recently, which could reduce the
availability of credit for new residential

have noted an increase in investors’ redemption

noted a less profitable outlook for new residential

expected to weigh on both developers’ demand for

Longer-term real government yields have
risen by as much as 85 basis points over 2026,
reaching their highest levels since the early
2000s (Graph A.1). Most of this increase reflects
higher real yields rather than changes in
longer-term inflation expectations, which have
remained broadly stable. The current increase is
part of a broader and more sustained rise in
longer-term yields since 2021 (see Box B:
Developments in the Neutral Rate of Interest).

Higher real yields do not necessarily imply tighter
financial conditions. Their effect on financial
conditions depends on what is driving the
increase and how changes in long-term rates are
transmitted through the economy.

Longer-term real government yields have risen sharply across advanced economies in recent months. Whether
this tightens financial conditions depends on why yields have risen. If yields have risen because markets expect
stronger growth, the prospect of higher earnings and returns on investment can more than offset higher
discount rates, thereby supporting asset prices and investment. If they have risen because markets expect

more restrictive monetary policy or require a higher term premium, there is no such support and financial
conditions are likely to have tightened. The recent rise in yields appears to reflect a mix of factors. In Australia,
long-term yields have less direct influence on borrowing costs than in the United States because most
borrowing here is at variable rates. However, this does not necessarily mean weak transmission overall:
increases in foreign yields can still affect domestic financial conditions through asset prices and the exchange

rate. This Box examines the drivers of longer-term real yields, places the recent increase in historical context,
and considers how higher yields affect financial conditions in Australia.
What drives longer-term real yields?
Longer-term real yields reflect the expected path of future short-term real rates and a so-called ‘term
premium’. Increases in real yields can be grouped into three broad drivers, with the first two working through
expected policy rates:

1. stronger expected growth: markets may expect stronger economic growth, which can raise expected real
policy rates because the neutral rate is thought to have risen (e.g. because of higher productivity growth)
2. other policy repricing: markets could expect policy rates to stay higher for longer for reasons other than
stronger growth, such as when inflationary pressures lead markets to expect policy to remain restrictive (i.e.
above the neutral rate) for a time, such as during persistent adverse supply shocks

3. higher term premium: investors may demand greater compensation to hold longer-term bonds, above
what they would demand to hold a series of equivalent shorter-term instruments, while expectations for
growth and policy rates are little changed.
Box A: How Does an Increase in Longer-term Real
Yields Affect Financial Conditions?
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 22

### Page 26

These drivers can have different implications for financial conditions. When higher yields reflect stronger
expected growth, higher expected earnings and returns on investment can more than offset higher discount
rates, thereby supporting asset prices. By contrast, when yields rise because of expectations of more restrictive
monetary policy or a higher term premium, there is no comparable offset and financial conditions are more
likely to tighten. In practice, yield movements can reflect a combination of these drivers.

Putting the recent increase in historical context.
The increase in real yields this year is notable, but not unprecedented. It is comparable in size with several
previous episodes, including the dot-com boom, the pre-global financial crisis (GFC) expansion and the
2023 term premium sell-off (Graph A.2). The drivers of these episodes differed, however. Table A.1 classifies past
episodes by our assessment of their predominant driver, based on contemporaneous market developments

and revisions to consensus forecasts of activity, inflation and policy rates. Several episodes reflected more than
one driver.

substantially, while long-term inflation forecasts were broadly

stable.

The dot-com boom provides the clearest example of higher yields accompanying stronger expected
longer-term growth. The pre-GFC and 2016–2018 synchronised growth periods were also associated with
strong asset prices and economic activity, but forecasts at the time suggest that stronger growth was expected
to be cyclical rather than structural. By contrast, the taper tantrum and 2023 term premium sell-off were
associated with weaker asset prices and little change in long-term growth expectations, consistent with a

larger role for term premia.

Historical experience also provides a useful reminder that the expectations underpinning market
pricing are not always realised. In some cases, periods of strong optimism about future growth have given
way to sharp falls in risk asset prices, notably following the dot-com boom and the expansion preceding the

GFC. In others, interest rate markets may be slow to recognise lasting improvement in growth prospects.
The current episode appears to reflect a combination of these drivers. In the United States, higher growth
expectations appear to have contributed: rising yields have coincided with stronger earnings expectations and
higher equity prices, particularly in AI-sensitive sectors, while credit spreads have remained near historical lows
(Graph A.3). Expectations of a higher path for policy rates have also contributed. Increased debt issuance and

ongoing quantitative tightening, alongside an investor base that is becoming more sensitive to expected
returns, also appear to be placing upward pressure on term premia.
?Box A  |  How Does an I ncrease in L onger-term Real Yields Affect Financial  Conditions ??????How Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial ConditionsHow Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial ConditionsHow Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial ConditionsHow Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial ConditionsHow Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial ConditionsHow Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial Conditions

### Page 28

In Australia, however, the rise in yields has not been accompanied by a comparable improvement in
growth expectations priced into financial assets. That is consistent with the much smaller AI-related sector
here, and the less pronounced reassessment of the broader domestic economic outlook. Instead, the rise in
Australian yields appears to reflect expectations of a higher path for the cash rate and spillovers from global
yields (see Chapter 1: Financial Conditions and Box B: Developments in the Neutral Rate of Interest). As a small

open economy, Australia is affected by global movements in longer-term yields, including in neutral rates and
term premia, even when domestic fundamentals have changed little.

Sources: Bloomberg; Federal Reserve Bank of Cleveland; ICE Data.
US Financial Conditions and Increases in Real Yields*
How do longer-term real yields affect financial conditions in Australia?
An increase in longer-term real yields can be transmitted into broader financial conditions through
borrowing costs, asset prices, investment and the exchange rate. The overall effect on financial conditions

depends on why yields have risen, with the three drivers discussed above having different implications
for Australia.
When higher global yields reflect stronger expected growth, the implications for Australian financial
conditions are ambiguous. If stronger growth is also expected for Australia, higher expected earnings and
returns on investment may offset the effects of higher discount rates. But if the improvement in growth
expectations is concentrated abroad, such as from AI-related investments, Australia may import some of the

rise in long-term yields without receiving the same benefit of higher expected growth. In that case, the
increase in global yields could tighten Australian financial conditions. A persistent productivity or growth
differential (in favour of the United States for example) would also be expected to put downward pressure on
the real exchange rate, which would provide some offset to the tightening in domestic financial conditions.
Similarly, stronger overseas activity could provide some offset by lifting domestic incomes (through trade and

commodity-price channels).

When higher yields reflect expectations of more restrictive monetary policy, financial conditions are
more likely to tighten. Expected borrowing costs rise, while higher discount rates weigh on asset prices and
investment. If Australian policy rates are expected to rise relative to those abroad, a higher exchange rate could
tighten domestic conditions further.
When higher yields reflect a higher term premium, the direct effect on broader financial conditions

depends partly on the economy’s exposure to longer-term rates. Differences in financing structures can
therefore mean that the same rise in longer-term yields is transmitted differently across economies.
Australian household borrowing is concentrated in variable-rate mortgages, making borrowing costs
less directly sensitive to term premia than in the United States (Table A.2). Thirty-year fixed-rate mortgages
are common in the United States, which makes new borrowing rates sensitive to term premia but also means

that changes in term premia can take a long time to affect average outstanding rates on mortgages. The
differences are less clear-cut for business financing. Higher discount rates may have a larger direct effect on
aggregate US equity indices because they are more heavily weighted towards technology companies, whose
valuations depend heavily on earnings many years ahead.

Response to Higher US Term Premia
While differences in financial structures affect the direct transmission of higher yields to the economy,
the overall effect on financial conditions also depends on broader responses in asset prices and other
financial variables. Model-based estimates, using data from 1995 onwards, suggest that US

term-premia-driven increases in longer-term yields have, on average, been associated with higher borrowing
costs and lower equity and housing prices in both Australia and the United States (Graph A.4).1
Endnotes
The responses in Graph A.4 are estimated using local projections (Jordà O (2005), ‘Estimation and Inference of Impulse Responses
by Local Projections’, American Economic Review, 95(1), pp 161–182), with the change in the US 10-year term premium (Kim D and

J Wright (2005), ‘An Arbitrage-Free Three-Factor Term Structure Model and the Recent Behavior of Long-Term Yields and
Distant-Horizon Forward Rates’, FEDS No 2005-33) used as an instrument for each market’s 10-year government bond yield.
Because US term premium movements can reflect a range of underlying shocks, the estimates capture average historical
associations rather than the effects of a specific structural shock. In addition, the term premium estimates we use are

model-based decompositions that cannot always distinguish perfectly between increases in yields stemming from the term
premium and those from other drivers such as growth expectations. For a small open economy like Australia, the instrumental
variable approach nonetheless helps to separate movements in yields from developments affecting both yields and financial
conditions (this interpretation is less straightforward for the United States). This means that some of the results in Graph A.4 – for

instance, the slight increase in mortgage rates and housing credit – may reflect in part higher expected global growth instead of
a ‘pure’ increase in the US term premium. The confidence intervals around these estimates are wide, particularly for Australia.

### Page 31

Long-term bond yields in advanced economies have increased substantially recently, continuing their upward
trend in the post-pandemic period (see Chapter 1: Financial Conditions). Part of this increase is likely to reflect a
rise in the neutral rate of interest – the level of the short-term real interest rate that balances saving and
investment and neither stimulates nor dampens demand once temporary influences on the economy have
faded. The neutral rate can help inform assessments of the stance of monetary policy. All else equal, a higher

neutral rate implies that a given level of the cash rate is less restrictive than otherwise. However, the neutral rate
is not directly observed and estimates are subject to considerable uncertainty both about its level and
underlying drivers. There is also uncertainty about estimates of other influences on long-term interest rates,
notably term premia, which have also contributed to the increase in longer-term sovereign bond yields. This
Box discusses the concept of the neutral rate, its possible drivers and the associated uncertainties.

What is the neutral interest rate?
The neutral rate of interest can refer to short-run or long-run concepts, each of which has different
connections to forecasts and policy. The long-run neutral rate is the level of the cash rate that neither
stimulates nor dampens demand once temporary shocks and structural forces have played out. At that point,
demand and potential supply will be in balance, with full employment and inflation at target. It is also

consistent with global saving and investment being in balance. Some of the RBA’s Australian neutral rate
models lend themselves more to this long-run concept of neutral.1
However, the long-run neutral rate may not correspond to the appropriate setting for the cash rate in
the short term because monetary policy may need to respond to temporary influences on activity and
inflation. The short-run neutral rate is a related concept that aims to capture these forces. This is the cash rate

that neither stimulates nor dampens demand over the next few years, taking account of ongoing shocks and
structural changes.2 The short-run neutral rate is less commonly estimated in the literature, although some
models in the RBA’s suite provide signals about shorter-run developments, particularly those based on
forward-looking financial market pricing. In practice, it is often unclear in real time whether developments are
temporary or persistent, meaning that short-run and long-run concepts of neutral can overlap. Consequently,

even models that are intended to represent a long-run forecast of the policy rate may partly capture
shorter-run trends.
The RBA’s forecasts set out our assessment of how shorter-run developments in financial and economic
conditions are likely to affect inflation and unemployment, which helps inform the Monetary Policy
Board’s assessment of the appropriate stance of monetary policy. The forecasts also attempt to capture
the direct impact on the economic outlook of structural change, for example by factoring in estimates of the

non-accelerating inflation rate of unemployment and potential output. Estimates of neutral rates provide a
useful cross-check of the forecasts. These estimates are shaped by many of the same developments but infer
their effects from economic data and market prices using different techniques. This provides an additional
perspective on developments in the economy but can also make it difficult to tell whether developments
driving the neutral rate estimates are reflected in the forecasts. The value of the cross-check comes from

understanding what the estimates imply about the stance of monetary policy and what is likely to be driving
these estimates.
When examining the neutral rate and its drivers, it is important to consider both domestic and global
factors. Australia is a small open economy, which means that we are price takers in global financial (and other)
markets given the sizeable influence of capital flows on domestic financial conditions. Hence, shifts in the

Nominal Neutral Rate
global balance of saving and investment will have a large influence on the neutral rate in Australia, though they

are not the only influence (see below). Global forces relevant to the neutral rate include productivity growth,
demographic trends and government debt levels.
Estimates of neutral rates have increased in the post-pandemic period
and there are a range of possible explanations.
Estimates of the neutral rate in Australia have increased over recent years to be above their
pre-pandemic levels, partly reversing a long-run decline (Graph B.1). A similar trend is evident in many

advanced economies, including the United States.3 Although the increase over recent years is relevant to the
assessment of the current stance of monetary policy, understanding it requires considering why neutral rate
estimates declined over the decades leading up to the pandemic. The research literature has put forward two
competing explanations. First, the decline could reflect slow-moving structural factors, such as demographics,
weak productivity growth and inequality, that caused ‘secular stagnation’. Second, the particularly sharp decline

in neutral rate estimates in the 2010s could be picking up more temporary factors, such as the need to repair
balance sheets in the aftermath of the global financial crisis (GFC).4
The first explanation, secular stagnation, argues
that households and businesses increasingly
wished to save more, relative to firms’ desired
investment, placing downward pressure on
interest rates.5 Three possible causes identified in
the literature are demographic change, weak

productivity growth and rising inequality globally.
Population ageing and rising inequality can increase
desired saving, while weaker productivity growth can
reduce desired investment. Together, these factors
will put downward pressure on interest rates. At the
same time, a number of governments globally ran
substantial fiscal deficits, including because of
increased social security expenditure to support
ageing populations. The literature argues that the

decline in neutral rates would probably have been
even larger without these fiscal deficits.
A second explanation is that the unsustainable credit boom in the 2000s and the balance sheet
damage caused by the resulting GFC placed persistent, but ultimately temporary, downward pressure
on interest rates.6 This literature argues that many of the slow-moving structural factors associated with
secular stagnation do not align with either the steep decline in estimated neutral rates during the 2010s or

their subsequent increase. For example, the working-age population has declined relative to the old-age
population in the OECD in a relatively steady way that does not match the sharper decline and then rebound in
neutral rate estimates. Instead, this literature argues that high levels of household and business debt and large
falls in asset prices led households, businesses and financial intermediaries to spend a prolonged period
repairing their balance sheets. This desire to increase private saving and reduce private investment worked to

lower neutral rates. As the effects of the GFC faded and balance sheets were repaired, this downward influence
on neutral should have dissipated.
While the estimates presented above suggest that neutral interest rates have increased materially
since the pandemic, the drivers of that increase remain uncertain. The increase over recent years is
broadly consistent with explanations that emphasise the fading effects of the GFC, but it could also reflect

Neutral Rate – Fundamental Drivers
changes in longer-running structural factors (Graph B.2; Table B.1). For example, the post-pandemic rise in
neutral rate estimates has coincided with a rapid expansion in AI-related investment and stronger US
productivity growth than in the 2010s. Higher expected productivity can raise the expected return on

investment and encourage businesses to invest more, placing upward pressure on neutral rates. Large and
persistent fiscal deficits in advanced overseas economies could also be putting upward pressure on neutral rate
estimates by reducing public saving. As a share of GDP, post-pandemic deficits in the United States and
elsewhere have been larger than in the late 2010s or before the GFC.
The magnitude of these effects remains highly
uncertain.7 Structural changes, such as in expected

future productivity, are hard to measure and do not
necessarily occur gradually nor independently of
other forces. Newer and ongoing structural changes
are likely to be evolving in their effect on neutral
rates. The effects of climate change, for example, are
ambiguous: additional investment in renewables,
including to meet the energy requirements of AI,
could put upward pressure on neutral rates, whereas
the higher returns required by investors to

compensate for physical climate risks could work in
the opposite direction.8 Globally, government deficits
have also continued to increase, and political and
geopolitical factors could sustain this trend. At the
same time, ageing populations and rising inequality
continue to weigh on neutral rates.

### Page 34

The global neutral rate is not the only driver of the neutral rate in
Australia.
As mentioned above, global forces have a strong influence on the Australian neutral rate because of
Australia’s role as a small open economy. However, they do not determine it completely. One study
suggests that changes in US neutral rates explain around half of the variation in Australian neutral rate
estimates.9 In theory, once the economy is in balance, shocks have dissipated and monetary policy is therefore

no longer responding to domestic or international shocks, the neutral rate in a small open economy should
equal the global neutral rate plus a risk premium. This risk premium could reflect impediments to the free flow
of capital or differences in the compensation that domestic and international investors require for risk.
There remain considerable uncertainties about both the level and drivers
of the neutral rate, which are consequential for policy.

Although neutral interest rates are an important conceptual framework for considering the stance of
policy, they cannot be observed directly and are difficult to measure. Different approaches to measuring
neutral rates offer different perspectives. Some approaches infer neutral from financial market prices and some
are estimated by examining the evolution of macroeconomic variables. The RBA accounts for this uncertainty
by examining a range of models. Another source of uncertainty arises from how these statistical models are

estimated, meaning that even a single model can produce a wide range of plausible estimates of the neutral
rate.10 And, as noted above, it is difficult to tell in real time how much each model reflects long-run trends
rather than shorter-term developments.
Reflecting this uncertainty, policymakers are cautious when interpreting (small) short-term moves.
Most models suggest that neutral rates have increased over recent years. However, the rise in long-term bond

yields over the year to date could reflect a number of factors other than changes in neutral rates, such as
expectations about future inflation and the additional compensation that investors require to hold longer-term
bonds (see Box A: How Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial Conditions). An increase in
long-term yields therefore does not, by itself, mean that the neutral rate has risen.
Even if neutral rates have increased, it is difficult to determine what has driven the increase. The

relevance of the long-run neutral rate estimates to current policy is mainly the information they contain about
underlying developments in the economy, and whether those are adequately reflected in the RBA’s forecasts.
The persistence and effects of these developments matter for the appropriate setting of policy over the next
few years, as captured by the short-run concept of neutral.
As an example, suppose the emergence of AI technologies leads to a modest increase in global productivity in

the long term. This could place some upward pressure on long-run neutral rate estimates without necessarily
significantly affecting near-term inflation or the appropriate cash rate today. However, if AI also increased
near-term aggregate demand by increasing investment in data centres and related infrastructure, then the
appropriate policy response could be to increase the cash rate by more than the change in the long-run
neutral rate. Given the uncertainty around both the extent of any increase in neutral rates and the forces

driving it, the possibility of a higher neutral rate is partially embedded in the current forecasts and is otherwise
being monitored as a source of uncertainty for the outlook (see Chapter 3: Outlook).

The RBA will continue to develop our understanding of the structural drivers of neutral rates and the degree to
which they are likely to have implications over different horizons for the appropriate setting of monetary policy.
Key areas for research include connecting movements in the neutral rate suite to fundamental economic
drivers, including better understanding the ‘wedge’ between domestic and global neutral rates.
